8.1
The discovery of bf8
In this subsection, we show how this extraordinary signature bf8 was discovered. We use the notation
bf8 since we consider the problem Holant(̸=2| bF) for complex-valued bF satisfying ars. We prove
that if bF contains an 8-ary signature bf where bf /∈b
O⊗, then Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard or bf8 is
realizable from bf (Theorem 8.5).
Remember that D = {̸=2}. Then D⊗= {λ · (̸=2)⊗n | λ ∈R\{0}, n ⩾1} is the set of tensor
products of binary disequalities ̸=2 up to a nonzero real scalar. If for all pairs of indices {i, j},
b∂ij bf ∈D⊗, then we say bf ∈bR
D⊗. Clearly, if bf ∈D⊗and bf has arity greater than 2, then bf ∈bR
D⊗.
We ﬁrst show the following result for signatures of arity at least 8.
Lemma 8.1. Let bf /∈b
O⊗be a signature of arity 2n ⩾8 in bF. Then,
• Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard, or
• there is a signature bg /∈b
O⊗of arity 2k ⩽2n −2 that is realizable from bf, or
• there is an irreducible signature c
f∗∈bR
D⊗of arity 2n that is realizable from bf.
Proof. Since bf /∈b
O⊗, bf ̸≡0. Again, we may assume that bf is irreducible. Otherwise, by factoriza-
tion, we can realize a nonzero signature of odd arity and we get #P-hardness by Theorem 2.25, or we
can realize a signature of lower even arity that is not in b
O⊗and we are done. Under the assumption
that bf is irreducible, we may further assume that bf satisﬁes 2nd-Orth by Lemma 4.4. Consider
signatures b∂ij bf for all pairs of indices {i, j}. If there exists a pair {i, j} such that b∂ij bf /∈b
O⊗, then
let bg = b∂ij bf, and we are done. Thus, we may also assume that bf ∈bR b
O⊗.
69

If for all pairs of indices {i, j}, we have b∂ij bf ≡0. Then, by Lemma 2.10, bf(α) = 0 for all α with
wt(α) ̸= 0 or 2n. Since f ̸≡0 and by ars, | bf(⃗02n)| = | bf(⃗12n)| ̸= 0. Clearly, such a signature does
not satisfy 2nd-Orth. Contradiction. Thus, without loss of generality, we assume that b∂12 bf ̸≡0.
Since b∂12 bf ∈b
O⊗, without loss of generality, we may assume that in the UPF of b∂12 bf, variables
x3 and x4 appear in one binary signature b1(x3, x4), x5 and x6 appear in one binary signature
b2(x5, x6) and so on. Thus, we have
b∂12 bf = bb1(x3, x4) ⊗bb2(x5, x6) ⊗bb3(x7, x8) ⊗. . . ⊗d
bn−1(x2n−1, x2n).
By Lemma 2.7, all these binary signatures bb1, bb2, . . . , d
bn−1 are realizable from f by factorization.
Note that for nonzero binary signatures bbi(x2i+1, x2i+2) (1 ⩽i ⩽n −1), if we connect the variable
x2i+1 of two copies of bbi(x2i+1, x2i+2) using ̸=2 (mating two binary signatures), then we get ̸=2
up to a scalar. We consider the following gadget construction on bf. Recall that in the setting of
Holant(̸=| bF), variables are connected using ̸=2. For 1 ⩽i ⩽n −1, by a slight abuse of names
of variables, we connect the variable x2i+1 of bf with the variable x2i+1 of bbi(x2i+1, x2i+2) using
̸=2. We get a signature bf′ of arity 2n. (Note that, as a complexity reduction using factorization
(Lemma 2.7), we can only apply it a constant number of times. However, the arity 2n of bf is
considered a constant, as bf ∈bF, which is independent of the input size of a signature grid to the
problem Holant(̸=2| bF).) We denote this gadget construction by G1 and we write bf′ as G1 ◦bf.
G1 is constructed by extending variables of bf using binary signatures realized from b∂12 bf. It does
not change the irreducibility of bf. Thus, bf′ is irreducible since bf is irreducible. Similarly, we may
assume that bf′ ∈bR b
O⊗. Otherwise, we are done.
Consider the signature b∂12 bf′. Since the above gadget construction G1 does not touch variables
x1 and x2 of f, G1 commutes with the merging gadget b∂12. (Succinctly, the commutativity can be
expressed as b∂12 bf′ = b∂12(G1 ◦bf) = G1 ◦b∂12 bf.) Thus, b∂12 bf′ can be realized by performing the gadget
construction G1 on b∂12 bf, which connects each binary signature bbi(x2i+1, x2i+2) in the UPF of b∂12 bf
with another copy of bbi(x2i+1, x2i+2) (in the mating fashion). Thus, each binary signature bbi in
b∂12 bf is changed to ̸=2 up to a nonzero scalar after this gadget construction G1. After normalization
and renaming variables, we have
b∂12 bf′ = (̸=2)(x3, x4) ⊗(̸=2)(x5, x6) ⊗(̸=2)(x7, x8) ⊗. . . ⊗(̸=2)(x2n−1, x2n).
(8.2)
Thus, b∂12 bf′ ∈D⊗. Moreover, for all pairs of indices {i, j} disjoint with {1, 2}, we have
b∂(ij)(12) bf′ ∈D⊗,
and hence b∂(ij)(12) bf′ ̸≡0.
(8.3)
A fortiori, for all pairs of indices {i, j} disjoint with {1, 2}, b∂ij bf′ ̸≡0.

We ﬁrst give a condition (Lemma 8.3) in which we can quite straightforwardly get the #P-
hardness of Holant(̸=| bf, bF) by 2nd-Orth given bf ∈bR
D⊗is an irreducible 8-ary signature.
Lemma 8.2. Let bf = a(1, 0)⊗2n + ¯a(0, 1)⊗2n + (̸=2)(xi, xj) ⊗bgh be an irreducible 2n-ary signature,
where 2n ⩾4 and bgh is a nonzero EO signature (i.e., with half-weighted support) of arity 2n −2.
Then, bf does not satisfy 2nd-Orth.
Proof. By renaming variables, without loss of generality, we may assume that {i, j} = {1, 2}.
For any input 00β ̸= ⃗02n of bf, we have bf(00β) = (̸=2)(0, 0) · bgh(β) = 0. Thus,
| bf00
12 |2 =
X
β∈Z2n−2
2
| bf(00β)|2 = | bf(⃗02n)|2.
On the other hand, since both (̸=2)(x1, x2) and bgh are nonzero EO signatures, (̸=2)(x1, x2) ⊗bgh
is a nonzero EO signature. Then, we can pick an input 01γ ∈Z2n
2
with wt(01γ) = n such that
bf(01γ) = (̸=2)(0, 1) · bgh(γ) ̸= 0. Since γ ∈Z2n−2
2
, and wt(γ) = n −1 ⩾1, there exists a bit γi in γ
such that γi = 0. Without loss of generality, we may assume that 01γ = 010γ′. Then,
| bf00
13 |2 ⩾| bf(⃗02n)|2 + | bf(010γ′)|2 > | bf(⃗02n)|2 = | bf00
12 |2.
Note that the constant λ for the norm squares must be the same for all index pairs {i, j} ⊆[2n] in
order to satisfy 2nd-Orth in Deﬁnition 4.1. Thus, bf does not satisfy 2nd-Orth.
Lemma 8.3. Let bf ∈bR
D⊗be an irreducible 8-ary signature in bF. If there exists a binary disequality
(̸=2)(xi, xj) and two pairs of indices {u, v} and {s, t} where {u, v}∩{s, t} ̸= ∅such that (̸=2)(xi, xj) |
b∂uv bf and (̸=2)(xi, xj) | b∂st bf, then Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard.
Proof. For all pairs of indices {i, j}, since b∂ij bf ∈D⊗, S (b∂ij bf) is on half-weight. By Lemma 2.10,
we have bf(α) = 0 for all wt(α) ̸= 0, 4, 8. Suppose that bf(⃗08) = a and by ars bf(⃗18) = ¯a. We can
write bf in the following form
bf = a(1, 0)⊗8 + ¯a(0, 1)⊗8 + bfh,
where bfh is an EO signature of arity 8.
Clearly, b∂ij bf = b∂ij bfh for all {i, j}. Then, bfh ∈bR
D⊗since bf ∈bR
D⊗. In addition, since there exists
a binary disequality (̸=2)(xi, xj) and two pairs of indices {u, v} and {s, t} where {u, v} ∩{s, t} ̸= ∅
such that (̸=2)(xi, xj) | b∂uv bfh, b∂st bfh, by Lemma 2.11, bfh ∈D⊗and (̸=2)(xi, xj) | bfh. Thus,

where bgh ∈D⊗is a nonzero EO signature or arity 6 since bfh ∈D⊗. By Lemma 8.2, bf does not
satisfy 2nd-Orth. Thus, Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard by Lemma 4.4.
For signatures in D⊗, we give the following property. Now we adopt the following notation for
brevity. We use (i, j) to denote the binary disequality (̸=2)(xi, xj) on variables xi and xj.
Lemma 8.4. Let bf ∈D⊗be a signature of arity at least 6. If there exist {u, v} ̸= {s, t} such that
(i, j) | b∂uv bf and (i, j) | b∂st bf, then (i, j) | bf.

Proof. For a contradiction, suppose that (i, j) ∤bf. Thus xi and xj appear in two separate disequal-
ities in the UPF of bf. Since bf ∈D⊗, there exists {ℓ, k} such that (i, ℓ) ⊗(j, k) | bf. By merging two
variables of bf, the only way to make xi and xj to form a binary disequality is by merging xℓand
xk. By the hypothesis of the lemma, {ℓ, k} = {u, v} = {s, t}. Contradiction.

The following Lemma shows that bf8 gives non- bB hardness (Deﬁnition 6.7).
Lemma 8.6. Holant(̸=2| bf8, bF) is #P-hard if bF contains a nonzero binary signature bb /∈bB⊗.
Equivalently, Holant(f8, F) is #P-hard if F contains a nonzero binary signature b /∈B⊗.
Proof. We prove this lemma in the setting of Holant(̸=2| bf8, bF). If bb /∈b
O⊗, then by Lemma 5.1,
we get #P-hardness. Thus, we may assume that bb ∈b
O⊗. Then, bb has parity. We ﬁrst consider the
case that bb has even parity, i.e., bb = (a, 0, 0, ¯a). Since bb ̸≡0, a ̸= 0. We can normalize a to eiθ where
0 ⩽θ < π. Then ¯a = e−iθ. Since bb /∈bB, a ̸= ±1 and a ̸= ±i. Thus, θ ̸= 0 and θ ̸= π
2 .
81

signature denoted by bg. We rename variables x2, x3, x4 of bg to x1, x2, x3 and variables x6, x7, x8 to
x4, x5, x6. Then, bg has the following signature matrix

where bb1 = (eiθ1, 0, 0, e−iθ1), bb2 = (eiθ2, 0, 0, e−iθ2) and bb3 = (eiθ3, 0, 0, e−iθ3). Then notice that
bg000000 = e−iθ = bb1(0, 0) · bb2(0, 0) · bb3(0, 0) = ei(θ1+θ2+θ3),
and

e−i2θ = ei(θ1+θ2+θ3) · ei(θ1−θ2−θ3) = ei2θ1.
Also, notice that

and
bg010010 = eiθ = bb1(0, 0) · bb2(1, 1) · bb3(0, 0) = ei(θ1−θ2+θ3).
By multiplying them, we have
ei2θ = ei(θ1+θ2−θ3) · ei(θ1−θ2+θ3) = ei2θ1.
Thus, ei2θ = e−i2θ. Then, ei4θ = 1. Since, θ ∈[0, π), θ = 0 or π
2 . Contradiction. Thus, bg /∈b

Lemma 7.21, we get #P-hardness.

Similarly, we can show that bg′ /∈b

We go back to real-valued Holant problems under the Z-transformation. Consider the problem
Holant(f8, F). Remember that f8 = bf8. We observe that, by Lemma 8.6 the set {f8} ∪F is non-B
hard, according to Deﬁnition 6.7. Then if we apply Theorem 7.19 to the set {f8} ∪F we see that
Holantb(f8, F) is #P-hard. Now if we were able to show that B is realizable from f8 then we would
be done, since by Theorem 8.5, we either already have the #P-hardness for Holant(F), or we can
realize f8 from F, and thus the following reduction chain holds
Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(F).
Thus we get the #P-hardness of Holant(F) in either way.
However, since f8 has even parity and all its entries are non-negative, all gadgets realizable from
f8 have even parity and have non-negative entries. Thus, =−
2 , ̸=2 and ̸=−
2 cannot be realized from
f8 by gadget construction. In fact, it is observed in [15] that f8 satisﬁes the following strong Bell
property.
Deﬁnition 8.7. A signature f satisﬁes the strong Bell property if for all pairs of indices {i, j},
and every b ∈B, the signature ∂b
ijf realized by merging xi and xj of f using b is in {b}⊗.
8.2
Holant problems with limited appearance and a novel reduction
In this subsection, not using gadget construction but critically based on the strong Bell property of
f8, we prove that Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F) in a novel way. We deﬁne the following Holant
problems with limited appearance.
Deﬁnition 8.8. Let F be a signature set containing a signature f. The problem Holant(f⩽k, F)
contains all instances of Holant(F) where the signature f appears at most k times.
Lemma 8.9. For any b ∈B, Holant(b, f8, F) ⩽T Holant(b⩽2, f8, F).
Proof. Consider an instance Ωof Holant(b, f8, F). Suppose that b appears n times in Ω. If n ⩽2,
then Ωis already an instance of Holant(b⩽2, f8, F). Otherwise, n ⩾3. Consider the gadget ∂b
ijf8
realized by connecting two variables xi and xj of f8 using b. (This gadget uses b only once.) Since
f8 satisﬁes the strong Bell property, ∂b
ijf8 = b⊗3. Thus, by replacing three occurrences of b in Ωby
∂b
ijf8, we can reduce the number of occurrences of b by 2. We carry out this replacement a linear
number of times to obtain an equivalent instance of Holant(b⩽2, f8, F), of size linear in Ω.
Now, we are ready to prove the reduction Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F).
Note that if
Holant(f8, F) is #P-hard, then the reduction holds trivially.
For any b ∈B, if we connect a
variable of b with a variable of another copy of b using =2, we get ±(=2). Also, for any b1, b2 ∈B
where b1 ̸= b2 if we connect the two variables of b1 with the two variables of b2, we get a value 0.
Lemma 8.10. Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F).
Proof. We prove this reduction in two steps.
Step 1. There exists a signature b1 ∈B\{=2} such that Holant(b1, f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F).
We consider all binary and 4-ary signatures realizable by gadget constructions from {f8}∪F. If
a binary signature g /∈B is realizable from {f8}∪F, then by Lemma 8.6, Holant(f8, F) is #P-hard,
83

and we are done. If a binary signature g ∈B\{=2} is realizable from {f8}∪F, then we are done by
choosing b1 = g. So we may assume that all binary signatures g realizable from {f8}∪F are =2 (up
to a scalar) or the zero binary signature, i.e., g = µ · (=2) for some µ ∈R. Similarly, if a nonzero
4-ary signature h /∈B⊗2 is realizable, then we have Holant(f8, F) is #P-hard, by Lemma 6.8, as
Lemma 8.6 says the set {f8} ∪F is non-B hard. If a nonzero 4-ary signature h ∈B⊗2\{=2}⊗2 is
realizable, then we can realize a binary signature b1 ∈B\{=2} by factorization, and we are done.
Thus, we may assume that all 4-ary signatures h realizable from {f8} ∪F are (=2)⊗2 or the 4-ary
zero signature, i.e., h = λ · (=2)⊗2 for some λ ∈R.
Now, let b1 be a signature in B\{=2}.
We show that Holant(b⩽2
1 , f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F).
Consider an instance Ωof Holant(b⩽2
1 , f8, F).
• If b1 does not appear in Ω, then Ωis already an instance of Holant(f8, F).
• If b1 appears exactly once in Ω(we may assume it does connect to itself), then we may
consider the rest of Ωthat connects to b1 as a gadget realized from {f8} ∪F, which must
have signature λ · (=2), for some λ ∈R. Connecting the two variables of b1 by (=2) for every
b1 ∈B\{=2} will always gives 0. Thus, Holant(Ω) = 0.
• Suppose b1 appears exactly twice in Ω. It is easy to handle when the two copies of b1 form a
gadget of arity 0 or 2 to the rest of Ω. We may assume they are connected to the rest of Ωin
such a way that the rest of Ωforms a 4-ary gadget h realized from {f8} ∪F. We can name
the four dangling edges of h in any speciﬁc ordering as (x1, x2, x3, x4). Then
h(x1, x2, x3, x4) = λ · (=2)(x1, xj) ⊗(=2)(xk, xℓ)
for some partition {1, 2, 3, 4} = {1, j} ⊔{k, ℓ}, and some λ ∈R.
(Note that while we
have named four speciﬁc dangling edges as (x1, x2, x3, x4), the speciﬁc partition {1, 2, 3, 4} =
{1, j} ⊔{k, ℓ} and the value λ are unknown at this point.) We consider the following three
instances Ω12, Ω13, and Ω14, where Ω1s (s ∈{2, 3, 4}) is the instance formed by merging
variables x1 and xs of h using =2, and merging the other two variables of h using =2 (see
Figure 4 where h1 = h2 = (=2) and h = λ · h1 ⊗h2). Since h is a gadget realized from
{f8}∪F, Ω12, Ω13, and Ω14 are instances of Holant(f8, F). Note that Holant(Ω1s) = 4λ when
s = j and Holant(Ω1s) = 2λ otherwise. Thus, by computing Holant(Ω1s) for s ∈{2, 3, 4}, we
can get λ, and if λ ̸= 0 the partition {1, j} ⊔{k, ℓ} of the four variables. Thus we can get the
exact structure of the 4-ary gadget h. In either case (whether λ = 0 or not), we can compute
the value of Holant(Ω).
Thus, Holant(b⩽2
1 , f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F). By Lemma 8.9, Holant(b1, f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F).
Figure 4: Instances Ω1j, Ω1k and Ω1ℓ
84

Step 2. For any b1 ∈B\{=2}, we have Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(b1, f8, F).
We show that we can get another b2 ∈B\{=2, b1}, i.e., for some binary signature b2 ∈B\{=2, b1}
we have the reduction Holant(b2, b1, f8, F) ⩽T Holant(b1, f8, F). Then, by connecting one variable
of b1 and one variable of b2 using =2, we get the third signature in B\{b1, b2}. Then, the lemma
is proved. The proof is similar to the proof in Step 1. We consider all binary and 4-ary gadgets
realizable from {b1, f8} ∪F. Still, we may assume that all realizable binary signatures are of the
form µ · (=2) or µ · b1 for some µ ∈R, and all realizable 4-ary signatures are of form λ · (=2)⊗2,
λ · b⊗2
1
or λ · (=2)⊗b1 for some λ ∈R. Otherwise, we can show that Holant(b1, f8, F) is #P-hard or
we realize a signature b2 ∈B\{=2, b1} directly by gadget construction.
Then, let b2 be an arbitrary signature in B\{=2, b1}. We show that
Holant(b⩽2
2 , b1, f8, F) ⩽T Holant(b1, f8, F).
Consider an instance Ωof Holant(b⩽2
2 , b1, f8, F). If b2 does not appear in Ω, then Ωis already
an instance of Holant(b1, f8, F).
If b2 appears exactly once in Ω, then it is connected with a
binary gadget g where g = µ · (=2) or g = µ · b1.
In both cases, the evaluation is 0.
Thus,
Holant(Ω) = 0. Suppose b2 appears exactly twice in Ω. Again it is easy to handle the case if
the rest of Ωforms a gadget of arity 0 or 2 to the two occurrences of b2. So we may assume
the two occurrences of b2 are connected to a 4-ary gadget h = λ · (=2)⊗2, λ · b⊗2
1
or λ · (=2)⊗b1.
We denote the four variables of h by (x1, x2, x3, x4), by an arbitrary ordering of the four dangling
edges. Then h(x1, x2, x3, x4) = λ · h1(x1, xj) ⊗h2(xk, xℓ) where h1, h2 ∈{=2, b1}, for some λ and
{j, k, ℓ} = {2, 3, 4}. (Note that at the moment the values λ and j, k, ℓare unknown.) We consider
the following three instances Ω12, Ω13 and Ω14, where Ω1s (s ∈{2, 3, 4}) is the instance formed by
connecting variables x1 and xs of h using =2, and connecting the other two variables of h using
=2 (again see Figure 4). Clearly, Ω12, Ω13 and Ω14 are instances of Holant(b1, f8, F). Consider the
evaluations of these instances. We have three cases.
• If h1 = h2 = (=2), then Holant(Ω1s) = 4λ when s = j and Holant(Ω1s) = 2λ when s ̸= j.
• If h1 = h2 = b1, then Holant(Ω1s) = 0 when s = j. If M(b1) is the 2 by 2 matrix form for the
binary signature b1 where we list its ﬁrst variable as row index and second variable as column
index, then we have Holant(Ω1k) = λ · tr(M(b1)M(b1)T), and Holant(Ω1ℓ) = λ · tr(M(b1)2),
where tr denotes trace. For b1 = (=−
2 ) or (̸=+
2 ), the matrix M(b1) is symmetric, and the value
Holant(Ω1s) = 2λ in both cases s = k or s = ℓ. For b1 = (̸=−
2 ), M(b1)T = −M(b1), and we
have Holant(Ω1k) = 2λ, and Holant(Ω1ℓ) = −2λ.
• If one of h1 and h2 is =2 and the other is b1, then Holant(Ω1s) = 0 for all s ∈{j, k, ℓ}.
Thus, if the values of Holant(Ω1s) for s ∈{2, 3, 4} are not all zero, then λ ̸= 0 and the third case is
impossible, and we can tell whether h is in the form λ · (=2)⊗2 or λ · (b1)⊗2. Moreover we can get
the exact structure of h, i.e., the value λ and the decomposition form of h1 and h2. Otherwise, the
values of Holant(Ω1s) for s ∈{2, 3, 4} are all zero. Then we can write h = λ·(=2)(x1, xj)⊗b1(xk, xℓ)
or h = λ · b1(x1, xj) ⊗(=2)(xk, xℓ), including possibly λ = 0, which means h ≡0. (Note that if
λ ̸= 0, this uniquely identiﬁes that we are in the third case; if λ = 0 then this form is still formally
valid, even though we cannot say this uniquely identiﬁes the third case. But when λ = 0 all three
cases are the same, i.e., h ≡0.) At this point we still do not know the exact value of λ and the
decomposition form of h.
We further consider the following three instances Ω′
12, Ω′
13 and Ω′
14, where Ω′
1s (s ∈{2, 3, 4}) is
the instance formed by connecting variables x1 and xs of h using b1, and connecting the other two
variables of h using =2. (In other words, we replace the labeling =2 of the edge that is connected
85

to the variable x1 in each instance illustrated in Figure 4 by b1.) It is easy to see that Ω′
12, Ω′
13 and
Ω′
14 are instances of Holant(b1, f8, F). Consider the evaluations of these instances.
• If h1 = (=2)(x1, xj), then Holant(Ω′
1s) = 0 when s = j.
Also we have Holant(Ω1k) =
λ · tr(M(b1)2), and Holant(Ω1ℓ) = λ · tr(M(b1)M(b1)T). For b1 = (=−
2 ) or ̸=+
2 , the matrix
M(b1) is symmetric, and the value Holant(Ω1s) = 2λ in both cases s = k or s = ℓ. For
b1 = (̸=−
2 ), M(b1)T = −M(b1), and we have Holant(Ω1k) = −2λ, and Holant(Ω1ℓ) = 2λ.
• If h1 = b1(x1, xj), then Holant(Ω′
1s) = 4λ when s = j and Holant(Ω′
1s) = 2λ when s ̸= j.
Thus, by further computing Holant(Ω′
1s) for s ∈{2, 3, 4}, we can get the exact structure of h.
Therefore, by querying Holant(b1, f8, F) at most 6 times, we can compute h exactly. Then, we
can compute Holant(Ω) easily. Thus, Holant(b⩽2
2 , b1, f8, F) ⩽T Holant(b1, f8, F). By Lemma 8.9,
Holant(b2, b1, f8, F) ⩽T Holant(b1, f8, F). The other signature in B\{=2, b1, b2} can be realized by
connecting b1 and b2. Thus, Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(b1, f8, F).
Therefore, Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F).
Since Holantb(f8, F) ⩽T Holant(f8, F) and {f8} ∪F is non-B hard for any real-valued F that
does not satisfy condition (T), by Theorem 7.19, we have the following result.
Lemma 8.11. Holant(f8, F) is #P-hard.
Combining Theorem 8.5 and Lemma 8.11, we have the following result.
Lemma 8.12. If bF contains a signature bf of arity 8 and bf /∈b
O⊗, then Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard.
9
The Induction Proof: 2n ⩾10
Now, we show that our induction framework works for signatures of arity 2n ⩾10.
Lemma 9.1. If bF contains a signature bf of arity 2n ⩾10 and bf /∈b
O⊗, then,
• Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard, or
• a signature bg /∈b
O⊗of arity 2k ⩽2n −2 is realizable from bf.
Proof. By Lemma 8.1, we may assume that an irreducible signature c
f∗of arity 2n ⩾10 where
c
f∗∈bR
D⊗is realizable, and c
f∗satisﬁes ars. We show that c
f∗does not satisfy 2nd-Orth, and
hence we get #P-hardness.
For all pairs of indices {i, j}, since b∂ijc
f∗∈D⊗, S (b∂ijc
f∗) is on half-weight. By Lemma 2.10,
we have c
f∗(α) = 0 for all wt(α) ̸= 0, n, 2n. Suppose that c
f∗(⃗02n) = a and c
f∗(⃗12n) = ¯a by ars. We
can write c
f∗in the following form
c
f∗= a(1, 0)⊗2n + ¯a(0, 1)⊗2n + c
f∗
h.
where c
f∗
h is an EO signature of arity 2n ⩾10.
Clearly, ∂ijc
f∗= ∂ijc
f∗
h for all {i, j}. Then, c
f∗
h ∈bR
D⊗since c
f∗∈bR
D⊗. Since c
f∗
h is an EO
signature of arity at least 10 and c
f∗
h ∈bR
D⊗, by Lemma 2.11, we have c
f∗
h ∈D⊗. Recall that all
signatures in D⊗are nonzero by deﬁnition. Pick some {i, j} such that (̸=2)(xi, xj) | c
f∗
h. Then,
c
f∗= a(1, 0)⊗2n + ¯a(0, 1)⊗2n + bb∗(xi, xj) ⊗bg∗
h,
where bg∗
h ∈D⊗is a nonzero EO signature since c
f∗
h ∈D⊗. By Lemma 8.2, c
f∗does not satisfy
2nd-Orth. Thus, Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard by Lemma 4.4.
86

Remark: Indeed, following from our proof, we can also show that there is no irreducible signature
bf of arity 2n ⩾10 that satisﬁes both 2nd-Orth and bf ∈bR b
O⊗.
Finally, we give the proof of Theorem 1.2. We restate it here.
Theorem 9.2. Let F be a set of real-valued signatures. If F satisﬁes the tractability condition (T)
in Theorem 2.22, then Holant(F) is polynomial-time computable; otherwise, Holant(F) is #P-hard.
Proof. By Theorem 2.22, if F satisﬁes condition (T), then Holant(F) is P-time computable. Sup-
pose that F does not satisfy condition (T). If F contains a nonzero signature of odd arity, then by
Theorem 2.25, Holant(F) is #P-hard. We show Holant(̸=2| bF) ≡T Holant(F) is #P-hard when F
is a set of signatures of even arity. Since F does not satisfy condition (T), bF ̸⊆T . Since b
O⊗⊆T ,
there is a signature bf ∈bF of arity 2n such that bf /∈b
O⊗. We prove this theorem by induction on
2n.
When 2n ⩽8, by Lemmas 5.1, 5.2, 7.21, 8.12, Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard.
Inductively, suppose for some 2k ⩾8, if 2n ⩽2k, then Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard. We consider
2n = 2k + 2 ⩾10. By Lemma 9.1, Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard, or Holant(̸=| bg, bF) ⩽T Holant(̸=| bF)
for some bg /∈b
O⊗of arity ⩽2k. By the induction hypothesis, Holant(̸=| bg, bF) is #P-hard. Thus,
Holant(̸=2| bF) is #P-hard.

We would like to thank Professor Mingji Xia for pointing out that the strong Bell property can
be used to prove Lemma 8.9, which led to the inspiration to prove Lemma 8.10. Without this
inspiration this paper may languish for much more time. We would also like to thank Professor
Zhiguo Fu for many valuable discussions and veriﬁcation of parts of the proof. The proof of the
present paper is built on his substantial previous work. Despite their invaluable contributions, they
generously declined our invitation for co-authorship.
